The Thurston norm via spun-normal immersions
Daryl Cooper, Stephan Tillmann and William Worden

Abstract
A theory of transversely oriented spun-normal immersed surfaces in ideally triangulated 3–manifolds is developed in this paper, including linear functionals determining the boundary curves, Euler characteristic and homology class of these immersions. This is used to develop and implement an algorithm to compute the unit ball of the Thurston norm for cusped hyperbolic 3–manifolds of finite volume. As an application of independent interest, we give an upper bound on the minimal entropy of pseudo-Anosov maps of surfaces with number of cusps bounded linearly in genus.



keywords
3–manifold, Thurston norm, triangulation, ideal triangulation, normal surface, spun-normal surface, pseudo-Anosov, entropy, dilatation

\primaryclass

57K32, 57K31, 57K10

\makeshorttitle

1
Introduction

Let
M
be a cusped hyperbolic 3–manifold of finite volume. The motivation for this paper is to use an ideal triangulation of
M
and spun-normal surfaces to give an algorithm to compute the unit ball of the Thurston norm of
M.
A similar algorithm for closed 3–manifolds using closed normal surfaces was given by the first two authors in
[
3
]
, and finer structure results were obtained by Tollefson and Wang
[
26
]
.

To begin with, let
M
be the interior of the compact, orientable 3–manifold
\overline{M}
with boundary a finite union of tori, and let
\mathcal{T}
be an arbitrary ideal triangulation of
M.
Our main technical contribution is the realisation of certain transversely oriented normal
Q
–coordinates by transversely oriented immersed normal surfaces (see
Section

2
). This requires an adaptation of the
neat position
of
[
3
]
. The coordinates have the property that surfaces with the same coordinate may be topologically distinct and may have different boundary slopes, but they always have the same image in second homology and the same Euler characteristic. The
Q
–coordinates form a positive linear cone
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})
defined by linear equations arising from
shift conditions
, analogous to the conditions given by Dunfield and Garoufalidis
[
5
]
in the standard setting.
Based on the discussion by Garoufalidis, Hodgson, Hoffman and Rubinstein, we describe an Euler characteristic function on the space of spun-normal surfaces. This is given in
Section

3
using the existence of a generalised angle structure with special properties.

We describe a linear map
\mathcal{H}\co\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
in
Section

4.1
, which takes the coordinate of a transversely oriented immersion to the corresponding homology class.
Under the additional assumption that
M
is the complement of a link in a homology sphere, we show in
Section

4.2
how to determine the homology map using the boundary map of
[
24
]
. Finally, an algorithm to compute the unit ball of the Thurston norm is devised in
Section

4.3
, resulting in our main theorem as follows:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\,\overline{\!{M}}
be a compact, orientable 3-manifold with non-empty boundary and suppose that
M=\mathrm{int}(\,\overline{\!{M}})
has a complete hyperbolic structure of finite volume. Suppose a
0
–efficient ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
M
is given. Then there is an algorithm using transversely oriented normal surface theory to determine the unit ball of the Thurston norm on
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
.

The unit norm ball can also be computed (in principle) from Heegaard Floer homology
[
1
,
15
]
or twisted Alexander polynomials
[
7
]
. We are not aware of implementations of these algorithms. The implementation of our algorithm is based on
Regina

[
2
]
and
SnapPy

[
4
]
. Notes on implementation and computational results can be found in
Section

5
.

We apply our algorithm in
Section

6
to obtain a result concerning the entropy of pseudo-Anosov maps of cusped surfaces that is of independent interest. This result gives a partial answer to a question posed to us by Eriko Hironaka, which was originally asked by Mehdi Yazdi (see
[
31
, Question 4.13]
).

Let
S=\Sigma_{g,n}
be a surface of genus
g
with
n
punctures, and let
\varphi\in\mathrm{Mod}(S)
be a pseudo-Anosov mapping class. Let
\lambda_{\varphi}
denote the dilatation of
\varphi
, and define
l_{g,n}=\min\{\log(\lambda_{\varphi})\mid\varphi\in\mathrm{Mod}(S)\text{ is pseudo-Anosov}\}
.

Penner
[
19
]
has shown that
l_{g,0}\asymp\frac{1}{g}
, and that for any surface with
\chi(S)<0
:

\frac{\log 2}{12g-12+4n}\;\leq\;l_{g,n}

Combining this lower bound with the upper bound
l_{0,n}\leq\frac{2\log(2+\sqrt{3})}{n-3}
for
n\geq 4
of Hironaka and Kin
[
11
]
, it follows that
l_{0,n}\asymp\frac{1}{n}
. Note also that Penner’s bound implies that
\frac{c_{1}}{|\chi(S)|}\leq l_{g,n}
, for
c_{1}=\frac{\log 2}{6}
, as long as
\chi(S)<0
.

For fixed
g\geq 2
, Tsai
[
27
]
has shown that
l_{g,n}\asymp\frac{\log n}{n}
. Moreover, Tsai
[
27
]
shows that there exists a constant
c_{2}
such that for any
g\geq 2
and
n\geq 0
one has

l_{g,n}\leq c_{2}\cdot g\frac{\log|\chi(S)|}{|\chi(S)|}

Figure 1
:
Qualitative sketch of the region of the
(g,n)
-plane covered by the bound of
Theorem

2
(in blue), and the region covered by the bound of
[
31
, Theorem 1.2]
(in red).

Work of Yazdi
[
30
]
shows that for any positive real number
\alpha
, there exists a constant
c_{\alpha}
such that for any
g\geq 2
,
n\geq 0
there is the following lower bound:

\frac{c_{\alpha}}{g^{2+\alpha}}\frac{\log|\chi(S)|}{|\chi(S)|}\;\leq\;l_{g,n}

This is a strengthening of Penner’s bound in the case where
n\gg g
. The above work of Tsai implies that an analogous upper bound does not hold if we allow
n
to grow independently of
g.
However,
as an application of our new algorithm, we are able to make the following new observation in this line of work:

Theorem 2
.

Fix any
1>\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a constant
c_{\varepsilon}
depending on
\varepsilon
such that for all
g\geq 2
and for all
n
satisfying
\varepsilon g+2\leq n\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g+2
we have

l_{g,n}\leq\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{|\chi(S)|}

where
\chi(S)
is the Euler characteristic of
S=\Sigma_{g,n}
.

This theorem complements recent work of Yazdi
[
31
]
, in which it is shown that there exist positive constants
A,B
, and
C
so that for all
n\geq 1
and
g\geq Cn\log^{2}n
one has
\frac{B}{g}\leq l_{g,n}\leq\frac{A}{g}
. Notably, the constants in Yazdi’s bound are independent of
g
and
n
, as is the constant
c_{\epsilon}
in our bound. At first glance it may seem that our bound is an improvement on Yazdi’s upper bound, but we note that the set of all
(g,n)
to which both results apply is finite for any fixed
\epsilon
.
Figure

1
shows for fixed
\epsilon
the regions of the
(g,n)
-plane on which each result applies.

2
Transversely oriented spun-normal surfaces

A new idea in
[
3
]
for normal surfaces in closed 3–manifolds was the use of transversely oriented normal discs in order to define an orientation on a normal surface, and hence a map to the second homology of the 3–manifold. A key result was the construction of an immersed transversely oriented normal surface for each admissible coordinate. This was achieved through a placement of the transversely oriented normal discs that was called
neat position
. In this section, we generalise this set-up to spun-normal surfaces in ideally triangulated, orientable 3–manifolds with torus cusps. As in
[
24
]
, with a little more notation and additional cases, the results of this section can be generalised to arbitrary ends and non-orientable 3–manifolds.
All manifolds and maps are understood to be PL in this paper.

2.1
Ideal triangulation

The notation of
[
12
,
24
]
is used in this paper.
Let
M
be the interior of an orientable compact manifold with non-empty boundary consisting of a finite union of pairwise disjoint tori.
An
ideal triangulation
,
\mathcal{T},
of
M
consists of the union
\widetilde{\Delta}=\cup_{i}\Delta_{i}^{3}
of pairwise disjoint regular Euclidean 3–simplices of edge length 100, a set
\Phi
of isometries between pairs of faces, the natural quotient map
p\co\widetilde{\Delta}\to\widetilde{\Delta}/\Phi=P,
and a homeomorphism
h\co P\setminus P^{(0)}\to M,
between
M
and the complement of the 0–skeleton in
P.
The quotient space
P
is usually called a
pseudo-manifold
and the
end-compactification
of
M.

For brevity, we refer to a 3–manifold
M
imbued with an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}=(\widetilde{\Delta},\Phi,h)
as an
ideally triangulated 3–manifold
. Throughout, we assume that
P
and
M
are
oriented
, that
h
is orientation preserving, and that all singular 3–simplices in
P
are oriented coherently and the tetrahedra in
\widetilde{\Delta}
are given the induced orientation. There is a natural bijection between tetrahedra in
\widetilde{\Delta}
and singular 3–simplices in
P
.

A
normal corner
in a 1–simplex is an interior point of a 1–simplex. A
normal arc
in a 2–simplex is a properly embedded arc whose boundary consists of normal corners on distinct edges of the 2–simplex. A
normal disc
in a 3–simplex is a properly embedded triangle or quadrilateral whose vertices are normal corners on different edges of the 3–simplex. The boundary edges of a normal disc are thus normal arcs on distinct faces of the 3–simplex. The normal discs (arcs, corners) in
P
are the images of normal discs (arcs, corners) in
\widetilde{\Delta}.

A
normal isotopy
(resp.
homotopy
) of
P
is an isotopy (resp. homotopy) keeping each (singular) simplex of each dimension invariant.

Let
S
be a surface and
f\co S\to M
be a proper immersion. We say that
f
(or
S
) is
normal
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
if
f
is transverse to the 2–skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
and the pull-back of the intersection with the 2–skeleton is a cell decomposition of
S
consisting of triangles or quadrilaterals. In particular, each such
triangle or quadrilateral in
S
maps to a normal triangle or quadrilateral in
M,
and the cell decomposition of each connected component of
S
has at most finitely many quadrilaterals (but possibly infinitely many triangles). We often indicate that a normal surface has infinitely many triangles by calling it a
spun-normal surface
.

A
transversely oriented normal immersion

(S,\nu)\to M
is a normal immersion
f\co S\to M
with a transverse orientation
\nu_{i}
of each connected component
S_{i}
of
S
. Whence each normal disc in
S
inherits a transverse orientation. We now describe a special set of transversely oriented normal discs in a 3–simplex, before we return to a global analysis of such normal immersions.

2.2
Neat position in a 3–simplex

Let
\Delta^{3}
be an oriented regular Euclidean 3–simplex of edge length 100. We now describe a special set consisting of infinitely many normal discs with transverse orientation in
\Delta^{3}.
The concept of neat position naturally leads to a generalisation to transversely oriented spun-normal surfaces of the
shift
that Dunfield-Garoufalidis
[
5
]
introduced for ordinary spun-normal surface coordinates.

2.2.1
Transversely oriented normal discs

A
transversely oriented normal disc

(D,\nu_{D})
in
\Delta^{3}
is a normal disc
D
with a
transverse orientation

\nu_{D}
. We regard
\nu_{D}
as a function on the components of
\Delta^{3}\setminus D
that sends one component to
+1
and the other to
-1.
The former component is denoted by
\Delta^{3}(D,\nu_{D})
and the maximal subsimplex of
\Delta^{3}
contained in it is
dual
to
(D,\nu_{D})
and denoted
\sigma(D,\nu_{D})
. Analogous notions and notation apply to
transversely oriented normal arcs
on 2–simplices and
transversely oriented normal corners
on 1–simplices. Moreover, the transverse orientation of
D
induces transverse orientations on the normal arcs and normal corners in the boundary of
D.

Two transversely oriented normal discs are equivalent if there is a normal isotopy of
\Delta^{3}
that takes one disc with its transverse orientation to the other. An equivalence class is called a
transversely oriented disc type
. There is a natural bijective correspondence between transversely oriented normal disc types and non-empty, proper subsimplices of
\Delta.
Hence there are
14
equivalence classes. If
\sigma(D,\nu_{D})
is a 0–simplex (resp. 2–simplex), then
(D,\nu_{D})
is a
small
(resp.
large
)
triangle
.

When we refer to a
normal isotopy class
it is often convenient to be able to refer to it
as a
corner, arc or disc in the given simplex. As
\Delta^{3}
is a regular Euclidean simplex, a canonical choice is to represent the class of a normal corner by the barycentre of a 1–simplex, and the class of any other normal object as the Euclidean convex hull of its vertices, all placed at barycentres of 1–simplices. In particular, a normal triangle type is represented by an equilateral triangle and a normal quadrilateral type by a square.
From now on, whenever we refer to a normal isotopy class, we refer to its canonical representative.

2.2.2
Neat position

We say that
(D,\nu_{D})
is at distance
k
from
\sigma(D,\nu_{D})
if
D
is the intersection of
\Delta^{3}
with a plane and each vertex of
D
is at distance
k
from a 0–simplex of
\sigma(D,\nu_{D})
, see
Figure

2(a)
.

(a)

Discs at distance
k
.

(b)

Neat arcs on a 2–simplex. There are 5 quads in this tetrahedron.

Figure 2
:
The placement of transversely oriented normal discs is shown. In (b), gray arrows indicate the direction of measurement and the transverse orientation of corners, and lower left labels are symmetric with lower right labels (not shown). Similarly, upper right labels are symmetric with upper left labels (not shown). Dotted lines indicate the simplices at distance 5 that large triangles limit to.

Dual to each of the 0–simplices of
\Delta^{3},
we now place small triangles in
\Delta^{3}
at distances
\frac{1}{n}
from the 0–simplex for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
(here we use the convention that the smallest natural number is 1).

Dual to each of the 2–simplices of
\Delta^{3},
we place large triangles in
\Delta^{3}
at distances
5-\frac{1}{n}
from the 2–simplex for each
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Last, we pick a pair
\Delta^{1}_{1}
and
\Delta^{1}_{2}
of opposite 1–simplices of
\Delta^{3},
and for each
\Delta^{1}_{i}
we choose a positive integer (possibly zero)
n_{i}
. If
n_{i}\neq 0,
we place
n_{i}
transversely oriented quadrilaterals dual to
\Delta^{1}_{i}
at distance
2+\frac{1}{n}
from the 1–simplex for each
1\leq n\leq n_{i}.

The infinite collection of transversely oriented normal discs thus placed in
\Delta^{3}
is said to be in
neat position
. Note that on each 1–simplex, the transversely oriented corners of discs are within distance 5 from its endpoints, and the transverse orientation of all corners is towards the closest vertex. The transversely oriented corners with the same orientation have two accumulation points along a 1–simplex, namely a vertex of
\Delta
and the corner at distance 5 from the vertex. See
Figure

2(b)
.

On each 2–simplex, we have one infinite family of transversely oriented normal arcs for each proper non-empty subsimplex. The short arcs accumulate on the dual 0–simplex, and the long arcs accumulate on the long arc at distance 5 from the dual 1–simplex.

Let
\Delta^{2}
be a 2–simplex of
\Delta^{3}.

We describe a natural labelling scheme for the transversely oriented normal arcs on
\Delta^{2}
with the elements of
\mathbb{Z}.
Non-negative labels are given to short arcs and negative labels to long arcs. Consider the collection of all short arcs dual to a vertex
\Delta^{0}
of
\Delta^{2}.
The short arc furthest from
\Delta^{0}
is given label 0, the next furthest label 1, etc. Similarly, consider the collection of all long arcs dual to an edge
\Delta^{1}
of
\Delta^{2}.
The long arc closest to
\Delta^{1}
is given label
-1
, the second closest is given label
-2,
etc. Note that if no quadrilateral in
\Delta^{3}
has a short (resp. long) arc of the given type, then the short (resp. long) arc with label
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is at distance
\frac{1}{n+1}
(resp.
5+\frac{1}{n}
) from its dual simplex.

(a)

The labelling scheme for transversely oriented corners

(b)

The positive and negative faces
t_{+}
and
t_{-}

Figure 3
:
Setting up the translation for an oriented edge
\vec{{e}}

The labelling scheme now also induces labels to transversely oriented corners on a 1–simplex
relative to
a 2–simplex, as shown in
Figure

3(a)
. Note that for each normal isotopy class of transversely oriented normal corner, there is a bijection between the corners of this type and the integers, and that the labels of the transversely oriented normal corners along the edge respect the natural order of the integers. The labels of transversely oriented corners depend on the reference 2–simplex, as can be seen from the cases shown in
Figure

4
.

So for a given 1–simplex
e
of
\Delta^{3}
, there are two labellings for each transversely oriented corner
(c,\nu_{c})
. As the labels respect the linear order of the integers, the labellings for each transversely oriented corner differ by a translation
T_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern which depends only on the normal isotopy class of the normal corner and the following orientation convention. The transverse orientation
\nu_{c}
gives
e
an orientation, and we denote the thus oriented edge by

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e\hfil

. The orientation of
\Delta^{3}
induces orientations on the 2–simplices containing
e,
and these in turn induce opposite orientations on
e.
The face
t_{+}
that induces
\nu_{c}
is the positive face, and the other,
t_{-}
, is the negative face for

:
Translations

Suppose
\tau
is an arbitrary Euclidean 3–simplex and
\mathcal{F}
is an infinite collection of transversely oriented normal discs in
\tau.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is said to be in
neat position
in
\tau
if there is a homeomorphism

h\co\tau\to\Delta^{3}
that takes the
k
–skeleton of
\tau
to the
k
–skeleton of
\Delta^{3}
for each
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, and has the property that
h(\mathcal{F})
is in neat position in
\Delta^{3}.

This completes the local consideration for a single 3–simplex.

2.3.1
Neat position of normal subset in pseudo-manifold

Now suppose
p\co\widetilde{\Delta}\to\widetilde{\Delta}/\Phi=P
is the quotient map described above, and each 3–simplex
\Delta^{3}_{k}
in
\widetilde{\Delta}
contains an infinite collection
\mathcal{F}_{k}
of transversely oriented normal discs in neat position.

Note that if no
\mathcal{F}_{k}
contains transversely oriented normal quadrilaterals, then we can apply the convention of
Section

2.2.2
to each
\Delta^{3}_{k}
and choose isometries as face pairings. Hence in
P
we obtain infinite collections of embedded vertex linking surfaces with orientation towards the dual vertices, and infinite collections of immersed surfaces that are homotopic to vertex linking surfaces with orientation away from the dual vertices.

In the presence of quadrilateral discs, we need to apply isotopies to the
\mathcal{F}_{k}
so that they glue up across 2–simplices.
Suppose
\varphi\in\Phi
is a face pairing between faces
t_{i}
and
t_{j}
of the 3–simplices
\Delta^{3}_{i}
and
\Delta^{3}_{j}
in
\widetilde{\Delta}.
After possibly pre-composing
\varphi
with a normal isotopy, we may assume that
\mathcal{F}_{i}\cap t_{i}
is mapped bijectively to
\mathcal{F}_{j}\cap t_{j}.
Indeed, this isotopy is essentially determined by the labelling scheme for the transversely oriented normal arcs, and
we may assume that this normal isotopy is

(a)

supported in a small neighbourhood of the face
t_{i}
of
\Delta^{3}_{i},
and

(b)

is the identity on each 1–simplex except for the distance 5 neighbourhood of its endpoints.

It follows that there is an isotopy of
\widetilde{\Delta}
supported outside of a small neighbourhood
\text{nhd}(\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)})
of the 1–skeleton of
\widetilde{\Delta}
such that the image of
\cup_{k}\mathcal{F}_{k}\setminus\text{nhd}(\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)})
is the image of an immersed transversely oriented surface in
P\setminus p(\text{nhd}(\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)})).

Under what conditions can we ensure that the isotopies can be chosen to agree on the 1–skeleton?
When we identify two faces of 3–simplices in
\widetilde{\Delta}
, the natural bijection between the labelling schemes for transversely oriented normal arcs is the identity map. As we go around an edge of a 3–simplex, we have described a translation that gives a shift in this coordinate system arising from the quadrilaterals. Hence as we go around an edge in
P,
we obtain a successive application of translations that have to combine to the identity as we return to the initial starting point. To state this more formally,
let
e
be an edge in
P
and suppose
p^{-1}(e)=\cup_{j=1}^{k}e_{j}\subset\widetilde{\Delta}^{(1)}.
An orientation

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e\hfil

induces an orientation
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}_{j}
of each
e_{j}.
Then we require that

T_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern We will now describe this as a system of equations in the coordinate system used in computation.

2.3.2
Matching equations

Let
\square
and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\square\hfil

respectively denote the set of all normal isotopy classes
of normal quadrilaterals in
\mathcal{T}
and transversely oriented normal quadrilaterals in
\mathcal{T}
,
so that
|{\square}|=3t
and

where

x(S)(q)
counts the number of quadrilateral discs in
S
of type
q\in\square
.

The map

The
transversely oriented normal
Q
–coordinate

satisfies the
following two algebraic conditions.

First,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}(S,\nu)
is admissible.
A vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\square}
is
admissible
if

x\geq 0
, and for each tetrahedron
x
is non-zero
on at most one of its three quadrilateral types.
This reflects the fact that an embedded surface cannot contain
two different types of quadrilateral in the same tetrahedron.
We say that
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern is admissible if its image
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\square}
is admissible.

Second,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}(S,\nu)
satisfies a linear equation for each ideal
edge
e
in
M
and each orientation chosen on this edge. We call this a

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil

–matching equation
.
The equation arises from the fact that the surface
S
meets
e
locally in a 2–disc, and each transversely oriented normal disc in
S
induces the same orientation on
e.
Moreover, the link of
e
is like an equator for a neighbourhood of
e,
and the boundary of the 2–disc crosses this equator from north to south
as often as it crosses from south to north.
We now give the precise form of these equations.
Recall that
M
is oriented and all tetrahedra are given
the induced orientation; see
[
24
, §2.9]
for
details.

(a)

The abstract neighbourhood
B(e)

(b)

Positive slope
+1

(c)

Negative slope
-1

Figure 5
:
Slopes of quadrilaterals.

Consider the collection
\mathcal{C}
of all ideal tetrahedra meeting at an edge
e
in
M
(including
k
copies of a tetrahedron
\tau
if
e
occurs
k
times as an edge in
\tau
). We form the
abstract neighbourhood
B(e)
of
e
by pairwise identifying faces of tetrahedra in
\mathcal{C}
such that there is a well defined quotient map from
B(e)
to the neighbourhood of
e
in
M
; see
Figure

5(a)
for an illustration. Then
B(e)
is a ball (possibly with finitely many points missing on its boundary). We think of the (ideal) endpoints of
e
as the poles of its boundary sphere, and the remaining points as positioned on the equator. The ball
B(e)
obtains a natural orientation from
M
, and as in
[
24
, §2.4]
we give the equator the orientation induced from the southern hemisphere.

Let
\tau
be a tetrahedron in
\mathcal{C}
. The boundary square of a transversely oriented normal quadrilateral of type
(q,\mu)
in
\tau
meets the equator of
\partial B(e)
if and only it has a transversely oriented corner on
e
. In this case, it has a slope
{s(q,e)=\pm 1}
of a well–defined sign on
\partial B(e)
which is independent of the orientation of
e
and the transverse orientation of
q.
Refer to
Figures

5(b)
and
5(c)
, which show quadrilaterals with
positive
and
negative slopes
respectively. If
D
is a connected transversely oriented normal surface in
B(e)
that meets
e
, then
D
is a disc. Indeed,
D
is the join of
\partial D
to
D\cap e
. Now
\partial D
can be oriented such that each slope of a quadrilateral in
D
corresponds to the signed intersection number of
\partial D
with the equator. In particular, the total sum is zero.

Since tetrahedra in
M
can appear with multiplicity in
B(e)
, the same is true for normal quadrilateral discs.
We thus define the
total weight

\wt_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern The analogous result for unoriented

postulates the existence of a

in the conclusion of the above theorem can be chosen to be embedded, then it is obtained from the unique embedded representative of

is equivalent to the translation

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}}
being the identity map. This follows from the definition of slopes and weights of quadrilateral discs.

As in
Section

2.3.1
, we therefore construct an infinite normal subset in neat position in the end-compactification
P
of
M.
The face pairings give identifications between edges of normal discs, and hence a map from a transversely oriented surface
(S,\nu)\to M\subset P
that is an immersion.
Since the translation associated to each oriented edge in
P
is the identity, the cell decomposition of
S
is locally finite.

Now discard any component of
S
that does not contain any quadrilateral discs, and denote the resulting surface again by
S.
Hence
S
is non-empty has finitely many components. If
S
contains only finitely many large triangles, then
(S,\nu)\to M
is a properly immersed surface and clearly an embedding of each end (after choosing a suitable compact core).

Hence assume
S
contains infinitely many large triangles. These triangles accumulate on an immersed surface
F
consisting only of large triangles. We now describe an explicit homotopy of
(S,\nu)\to M
that takes these triangles to triangles accumulating at the 0–skeleton of
P.

Each component
F_{\mathfrak{v}}
of
F
can be viewed as the immersion of a vertex linking surface at distance
95
from a vertex
\mathfrak{v}\in P^{(0)}.
Choose a compact core of
S
that contains all quadrilateral discs. A normal homotopy of the map
(S,\nu)\to M
that fixes the compact core and all small triangles is uniquely determined by describing where the corners of large triangles are mapped. The large triangles are then mapped to the Euclidean triangles with these endpoints.
There is
1>\varepsilon>0
such that
N_{\varepsilon}(F_{\mathfrak{v}})
only contains large triangles not contained in the compact core. An oriented edge

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e\hfil

ending in
\mathfrak{v}
is naturally parameterised by
[0,100]
with
100
corresponding to
\mathfrak{v}.
There is a homotopy of

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e\hfil

which is the identity on
[0,5-\varepsilon],
takes
[5-\varepsilon,5]
to
[5-\varepsilon,100]
by the linear map
x\mapsto\frac{1}{\varepsilon}[(95+\varepsilon)x-95(5-\varepsilon)]
and maps
[5,100]
to
100.
This is the homotopy we apply to each corner
(c,\nu_{c})
of a large triangle, where
c\in e
and
\nu_{c}
induces the
opposite
orientation of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e\hfil$\crcr}}}.
The resulting map is a proper immersion, and it is an embedding of each end since any two large triangles in
S
close to a vertex will have pairwise disjoint interior.

This proves the claim that each solution is realised by a proper immersion with embedded ends. The converse part follows from the description of the matching equations.
∎

Remark 4

((Notation))
.

We usually write
\widetilde{\Delta}=\{\tau_{i}\}
and label the vertices of each
\tau_{i}
by
0_{i},1_{i},2_{i},3_{i}
with the natural order corresponding to the chosen orientation. A quadrilateral type partitions the vertices into two pairs, and a transverse orientation is specified by only indicating the pair of vertices on the positive side. Thus, the transversely oriented normal quadrilateral types in
\tau_{i}
are written in the form
q^{i}_{ab},
where
0\leq a<b\leq 3.

We simplify notation by writing
q_{ab}

in sums over all simplices or if
\tau_{i}
is understood.

2.4
Boundary curve map

Following
[
24
]
, it is straightforward to determine the transversely oriented boundary curves of a transversely oriented normal surface. The construction is essentially the same as in
[
24
]
, except that the boundary arcs of the quadrilaterals are partitioned into two sets. One set, the
inward boundary
, corresponds to the ends of the surface that consist of large triangles, and the other set, the
outward boundary
, corresponds to the ends that consist of small triangles.

The methods of
[
24
]
are applied independently to each set. Thus we obtain two boundary curve maps. The image of each is a union of pairwise disjoint embedded simple closed curves in
\partial\,\overline{\!{M}}
, but the two sets may intersect non-trivially, as happens in the example given in the next section.

For each vertex
\mathfrak{v}
of
\mathcal{T}
, let
N_{\mathfrak{v}}
be an open regular neighborhood of
\mathfrak{v}
, and let
B_{\mathfrak{v}}=\partial\,\overline{\!{N}}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. We may assume that the closures of the vertex neighbourhoods are pairwise disjoint.
Hence identify
\,\overline{\!{M}}
with
M\setminus(\cup_{\mathfrak{v}}N_{\mathfrak{v}})
, so that the vertex links
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
are components of
\partial\,\overline{\!{M}}
. Fix
\mathfrak{v}
and let
\gamma
be an oriented normal closed curve in
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
. We start by constructing two linear functionals
w_{\gamma}^{+}
and
w_{\gamma}^{-}
on
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})
by defining them on quadrilaterals, then extending linearly. To this end, let
\Delta^{3}
be a tetrahedron in
\mathcal{T}
, and let
q
be a quadrilateral in
\Delta^{3}
. For any normal arc
\mathfrak{a}
in a face of
\Delta^{3}
there is a natural transverse orientation, pointing into the triangle cut off by
\mathfrak{a}
. A normal arc
\mathfrak{a}
in
\partial q
is in the
outward boundary

\partial q^{+}
if the two transverse orientations on
\mathfrak{a}
agrees with the transverse orientation on
q
, and is in the
inward boundary

\partial q^{-}
otherwise. Now orient
q
in such a way that the new orientation, taken with the transverse orientation of
q
, induces a left-handed orientation on
M
(see
Figure

6(a)
). This orientation of
q
induces an orientation on
\partial q
, and in particular on
\mathfrak{a}
. Let
\mathfrak{t}
be the unique normal triangle in
\Delta^{3}\cap\partial\,\overline{\!{M}}
whose boundary contains an arc
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
normally isotopic to
\mathfrak{a}
. Orient
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
so that its orientation agrees with that of
\mathfrak{a}
. Recall that for such an arc
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
and curve
\gamma
, the algebraic intersection number
\iota(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}},\gamma)
is
+1
if
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
crosses
\gamma
from left to right, and
-1
otherwise (see
Figure

6
).
Now define

w^{+}_{\gamma}(q)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\partial q^{+}}\iota(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}},\gamma)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptw^{-}_{\gamma}(q)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\partial q^{-}}\iota(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}},\gamma)

where the algebraic intersection number is defined with respect to the transverse orientation on
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
pointing away from
\mathfrak{v}
. Note that if
\mathfrak{t}
is not a triangle in the triangulation of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
, then
\iota(\gamma,\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}})=0
since
\gamma\subset B_{\mathfrak{v}}
. Letting
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern A vertex loop

:
(a): The outward boundary
\partial q^{+}
of
q
is shown in green, and the inward boundary
\partial q^{-}
is shown in red. If
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is in
\partial q^{+}
, a curve
\gamma
crossing through
\mathfrak{t}
contributes
+1
to
w_{\gamma}^{+}(q)
if it enters via the edge
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
, and contributes
-1
if it leaves via
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Similarly for
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
in
\partial q^{-}
, but with signs switched. (b): For a loop around a vertex
v
of the triangulation of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
, we must have
w_{\gamma}^{+}(\vec{x})=w_{\gamma}^{-}(\vec{x})=0
.

Now consider the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cong H_{1}(B_{\mathfrak{v}};\mathbb{Z})
which assigns to a vector
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
the class

satisfying
\iota(\gamma,\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}})=-x
and
\iota(\gamma,\mu_{\mathfrak{v}})=y
. This isomorphism extends uniquely to an isomorphism
\phi_{\mathfrak{v}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to H_{1}(B_{\mathfrak{v}};\mathbb{R})
. The map
\phi_{\mathfrak{v}}
takes basis vectors
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
to
\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}
and
\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}
, respectively. Finally, we define maps

\partial^{+}
and
\partial^{-}
are linear maps. The following proposition, which is a corollary of
[

, Proposition 3.3]
, shows that these maps are appropriately named:

Proposition 5
.

and
\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , where
\gcd(p_{i},q_{i})=1
for
i=1,2
. Then there are
k_{i}
components
\beta
of
f^{-1}(f(S)\cap B_{\mathfrak{v}})
such that
[f(\beta)]=p_{i}\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}+q_{i}\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}
in
H_{1}(B_{\mathfrak{v}};\mathbb{R})
, and
S
has
k_{1}+k_{2}
cusps on the cusp of
M
corresponding to
\mathfrak{v}
.

Proof.

Let
\triangle_{+}
denote the union of all small normal triangles in
f(S)
, and
\triangle^{-}
the union of all large normal triangles. It follows from the neat position construction that
\triangle^{+}
is embedded, and that every normal arc in
\partial\triangle^{+}
is in
\bigcup_{q\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\square\hfil$\crcr}}}}\partial q^{+}
. Fix a vertex
\mathfrak{v}
of
\mathcal{T}
, and let
\triangle_{\mathfrak{v}}^{+}
consist of the connected components of
\triangle^{+}
that are isotopic into
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
. The boundary
\partial\triangle_{\mathfrak{v}}^{+}
of
\triangle_{\mathfrak{v}}^{+}
consists of oriented arcs
\mathfrak{a}
along which quadrilaterals glue to small triangles. Each such arc is normally isotopic to an arc
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{t}}
in
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
, and these arcs taken together form a 1-chain
c
. It follows from the construction of
\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}^{+}
that
\partial_{\mathfrak{v}}^{+}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , where

is the class of

.
∎

2.5
Example: figure-8 knot

The figure-8 knot complement
M=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus 4_{1}
has Seifert surface a once punctured torus
S
. Since
M
is fibered with fiber
S
,
Theorem

14
does not guarantee the existence of an embedded spun-normal surface isotopic to
S
. Indeed, a simple calculation shows that there is no such spun-normal surface for the standard two-tetrahedron triangulation (see
[
13
]
). However, we demonstrate here that there is a properly immersed spun-normal surface
F
that is homologous to
S
, which is a thrice-punctured sphere (hence it is norm-minimizing). We discovered this using the computer program
Tnorm
, which is described in
Section

5
. On the other hand, in
Section

5
we also find a triangulation of the figure-8 knot complement by
five
tetrahedra, with respect to which the fiber
is
realized as an embedded spun-normal surface.

(a)

Tetrahedron 0

(b)

Tetrahedron 1

Figure 7
:
The figure-8 knot complement is triangulated by the two tetrahedra shown, with face pairings determined by edge colors and orientations.

shows Thurston’s famous triangulation of the figure-8 knot complement by two tetrahedra, which we denote by

3
Euler characteristic and generalised angle structures

We describe how one can determine the Euler characteristic of a transversely oriented normal immersion from its quadrilateral coordinate using certain angle structures that have
vanishing peripheral holonomy
. This material relies on
[
17
,
16
,
10
]
.

3.1
Angle structures and rotational holonomy

Let
e\in\mathcal{T}
be an edge in
M
and
q\in\square
be a quadrilateral type, contained in the 3–simplex
\Delta^{3}.

We now define the index
i(e,q).
Informally, this is the number of times
q

faces

e.

If
e
is not incident with
\Delta^{3}
, then we define
i(e,q)=0.
Otherwise, we let
i(e,q)
be the number of edges in the preimage of
e
in
\widetilde{\Delta}
that are incident with the preimage of
\Delta^{3}
and disjoint from the lift of
q
. It follows that
i(e,q)\in\{0,1,2\}
.

Definition 6
.

A
generalized angle structure
on a triangulated manifold
(M,\mathcal{T})
is a function
\alpha:\square\to\mathbb{R}
such that for each tetrahedron
\Delta^{3}\in\mathcal{T}

\sum_{q\subset\Delta^{3}}\alpha(q)=\pi

(3.1)

and for every edge
e\in\mathcal{T}

\sum_{q\in\square}i(\mathfrak{e},q)\alpha(q)=2\pi

(3.2)

In the case that
\alpha(q)\in[0,\pi]
for all
q\in\square
, we say that
\alpha
is a
semi-angle structure
. If
\alpha(q)\in(0,\pi)
for all
q\in\square
, then
\alpha
is a
strict angle structure
.

We can alternatively think of an angle structure
\alpha
as an assignment of dihedral angles to the tetrahedra of
\mathcal{T}
:
if
e
is an edge of
\Delta^{3}
, then the dihedral angle at
e
is
\alpha(q)
, where
q\subset\Delta^{3}
is the quadrilateral type disjoint from
e.
It follows that opposite edges of a 3–simplex have the same dihedral angle. Given a normal surface
S
, we then get an angle structure on
S
induced by
\alpha
as follows. If
t
is a normal triangle in a tetrahedron
\Delta^{3}
, then the dihedral angles of
\Delta^{3}
given by
\alpha
induces angle assignments on
t
: the vertex of
t
meeting the edge
e
of
\Delta^{3}
is assigned the angle at
e
. In the same way we also get angle assignments for vertices of normal quads. It follows from
Equation

3.1
that the three dihedral angles meeting a vertex of
\Delta^{3}
sum to
\pi
(equivalently, the total angle in any triangle of
S
is
\pi
), and it follows from
Equation

3.2
that the total dihedral angle around an edge of
\Delta^{3}
is
2\pi
(equivalently, the total angle around a vertex of
S
is
2\pi
).

For each vertex
\mathfrak{v}
of
M
let
N_{\mathfrak{v}}
be an open neighborhood of
\mathfrak{v}
, chosen so that for distinct vertices the neighborhoods are disjoint, and so that
B_{\mathfrak{v}}:=\partial N_{\mathfrak{v}}
is a vertex linking normal surface. Let
M^{c}=M\setminus\bigcup N_{\mathfrak{v}}
, so that
\partial M^{c}=\bigcup B_{\mathfrak{v}}
. For any isotopy class of simple closed curves on
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
, there is a representative
\gamma
that is
normal
with respect to the triangulation of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
by normal triangles. That is, for any normal triangle
t
of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
,
\gamma\cap t
is a properly embedded arc cutting off a corner of
t
.

Definition 7
.

Let
\gamma
be an oriented simple closed normal curve on
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
consisting of normal arcs
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
. The
rotational holonomy
of
\alpha
with respect to
\gamma
is defined to be

h_{\gamma}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i}\alpha_{i}

where
\alpha_{i}
is the angle associated to the corner of the triangle cut off by
a_{i}
, and
\epsilon_{i}=1
if
a_{i}
cuts off a corner to its left,
\epsilon_{i}=-1
if
a_{i}
cuts off a corner to its right.

We remark that if complex shape parameters are associate to the ideal tetrahedra
[
22
,
18
]
, then the rotational holonomy of a peripheral curve
\gamma
is the imaginary part of the logarithm of the derivative of its holonomy. If
h_{\gamma}(\alpha)=0
for every normal curve
\gamma
in
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
and every vertex
\mathfrak{v}
, then we say that
\alpha
has
vanishing peripheral rotational holonomy
.

The rotational holonomy has the following natural properties.

1.

h_{-\gamma}(\alpha)=-h_{\gamma}(\alpha),
where
-\gamma
denotes
\gamma
with opposite orientation;

2.

h_{\gamma+\sigma}(\alpha)\equiv h_{\gamma}(\alpha)+h_{\sigma}(\alpha)
modulo
2\pi
, where addition of curves is oriented Haken sum.

The following proposition is stated in
[
10
]
. We supply some details that are not explicitly mentioned in the proof and which are of particular interest for implementation.

Proposition 8
.

[
10
, Proposition A.2]

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an ideal triangulation of a manifold
M
with torus cusps. Then there exists a generalized angle structure on
\mathcal{T}
with vanishing peripheral rotational holonomy.

For example, if
M
admits a complete hyperbolic structure and
\mathcal{T}
is a geometric ideal triangulation of
M
, then the angle structure induced by the hyperbolic structure has vanishing peripheral rotational holonomy.

Proof.

The proof of
[
10
, Proposition A.2]
provides a generalised angle structure
\alpha
with the following property. For each peripheral torus
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
, one has a basis
\{\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}},\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}\}
with the property that
h_{\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}}(\alpha)=h_{\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}}(\alpha)=0.
To complete the proof of the proposition, it needs to be shown that
h_{p\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}+q\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}}(\alpha)=0
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}.

The argument given in
[
17
, pp.248-249]
implies that this is the case so long as the normal curves representing the basis elements are
simple
.
1
1

1

SnapPy will always choose such representatives for a basis when given a triangulation or a link diagram.
One way to choose such a basis algorithmically is as follows. Choose simple closed oriented curves
l_{\mathfrak{v}}
and
m_{\mathfrak{v}}
in the 1–skeleton of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}
with the property that
B_{\mathfrak{v}}\setminus(l_{\mathfrak{v}}\cup m_{\mathfrak{v}})
is a disc and
l_{\mathfrak{v}}\cap m_{\mathfrak{v}}
consists of a single vertex. The boundary of a small regular neighbourhood
N(l_{\mathfrak{v}})
(resp.
N(m_{\mathfrak{v}})
) of
l_{\mathfrak{v}}
(resp.
m_{\mathfrak{v}}
) consists of two normal curves, and we give these curves the orientation induced from the orientation of
N(l_{\mathfrak{v}})
as a subsurface of
B_{\mathfrak{v}}.
We then choose
\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}
(resp.
\mu_{\mathfrak{v}}
) to be the boundary component representing the same class in
H_{1}(B_{\mathfrak{v}})
as
l_{\mathfrak{v}}
(resp.
m_{\mathfrak{v}}
).
∎

3.2
Combinatorial Gauss–Bonet and Euler characteristic

Let
S
be a surface with cell
decomposition
C
. Here we allow
S
to be non-compact and to have boundary, and we allow the decomposition
C
to have infinitely many cells. Suppose further that there is a combinatorial angle structure on
(S,C)
, that is, a function that assigns to each corner
c
of each cell in
S
a real number
a(c)
, called the interior angle at
c.
We note that an interior angle can be negative or zero.
Following Thurston
[
22
, Chapter 13]
, the
following combinatorial quantities are defined:

•

If
c
is a
n
–gon in
C
, its
combinatorial hyperbolic
area

A(c)
is defined to be
(n-2)\pi-
(sum of interior angles
of
c
).

•

For each vertex
v
in
C
in the interior of
S
, define its

combinatorial cone curvarture
to be
K(v)=2\pi-
(sum of
angles at
v
).

•

For each boundary curve
s
of
S
, define a
combinatorial
boundary curvature
by
K_{g}(s)=\sum_{v\in s}\pi-
(angle
sum at
v
).

In the case when
S
is compact and
C
is finite, we have the following, which is a direct consequence of the definitions.

Theorem 9

(Combinatorial Gauß–Bonnet formula)
.

Let
S
be a compact surface with a finite cell decomposition
C

and an angle structure. Then:

2\pi\chi(S)=\sum_{v\in int(S)}K(v)-\sum_{c\in C}A(c)+\sum_{s\subseteq\partial S}K_{g}(s)

(3.3)

Now suppose
M
is the interior of an orientable compact manifold with non-empty boundary consisting of a union of tori, and
\mathcal{T}
an ideal triangulation with generalised angle structure.

If
f\co S\to M
is a normal immersion, then the Euler characteristic of
S
can be computed using the induced angle structure on
S
. If
S
is non-compact, choose a compact core
\overline{S}
of
S
with the property that the annuli in the closure of
S\setminus\overline{S}
only contain normal triangles.
The combinatorial area of each triangle is zero, and the combinatorial area of a quadrilateral
q
in a tetrahedron
\tau
is equal to twice the angle
\alpha(q)
of the edge of
\tau
which it misses. Moreover, the angle sum at each vertex in the cell decomposition equals
2\pi
. Thus one obtains:

2\pi\chi(S)=-\sum_{c\in C}A(c)+\sum_{s\subseteq\partial\overline{S}}K_{g}(s)=-2\sum_{q\in C}\alpha(q)+\sum_{s\subseteq\partial\overline{S}}K_{g}(s)

(3.4)

where
C
is the cell decomposition of
S
into normal disks. For
S
a closed surface we therefore have

\chi(S)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{q\in C}\alpha(q)

(3.5)

Let
\alpha
be a generalized angle structure on
\mathcal{T}
with vanishing peripheral rotational holonomy, as provided by
Proposition

8
.
For each transversely oriented normal quad
q\in\square
, define
\chi^{\ast}(q)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\alpha(q)
, and extend linearly over
Q(\mathcal{T})
to get a map
\chi^{\ast}:Q(\mathcal{T})\to\mathbb{R}
.

The proof of the following proposition now follows by observing that the boundary curvature always vanishes due to the vanishing peripheral rotational holonomy:

Proposition 10
.

[
10
, Proposition A.3]

Let
\chi^{\ast}:Q(\mathcal{T})\to\mathbb{R}
be the linear map defined above. If
f:S\to M
is a spun normal immersion, then
\chi^{\ast}(x(S))=\chi(S)
. Specifically,

\chi(S)=\sum_{q\in\square}\chi^{\ast}(q)\cdot x(S)(q)

(3.6)

We remark that the result was only stated for embeddings in
[
10
]
, but the proof also applies to immersions.
The following corollary is immediate.

Corollary 11
.

The map
\chi^{\ast}:\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by
\chi^{\ast}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern
