4
Homology map and the algorithm

Throughout this section,
M
is the interior of the compact, orientable 3–manifold
\overline{M}
with boundary a finite union of tori, and
\mathcal{T}
an ideal triangulation of
M.

We first define the homology map
\mathcal{H}\co\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
.
This is essentially the simplicial homology map described in
[
3
]
, adapted to our setting.
For a link complement in a rational homology 3–sphere, we can pick out a canonical basis and use the positive and negative boundary maps defined in
Section

2.4
to define the homology map. This is inspired by the examples of link complements in the 3–sphere analysed by Thurston
[
23
]
.
With the homology map and our definitions in hand,
Theorem

15
is
Theorem

1
with a more precise statement.

4.1
Simplicial homology map

For each vertex
\mathfrak{v}
of the pseudo-manifold
P
of
Section

2.1
, let
N_{\mathfrak{v}}
be an open regular neighborhood of
\mathfrak{v}
, and let
B_{\mathfrak{v}}=\partial\,\overline{\!{N}}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. As before, we assume that the regular neighborhoods are pairwise disjoint and identify
\,\overline{\!{M}}=P\setminus N,
where
N=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{v}\in P^{(0)}}N_{\mathfrak{v}}.
We also let
N^{\prime}\subset N
be a smaller regular neighbourhood of the 0-skeleton with the property that
\overline{N^{\prime}}\subset\Int(N).

We have isomorphisms
H_{2}(P;\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{g_{1}}H_{2}(P,N;\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{g_{2}}H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
, the first coming from the long exact sequence of the pair
(P,N)
and the fact that each connected component of
N
retracts to a point, and the second coming from the excision theorem and the fact that each connected component of
N\setminus N^{\prime}
retracts to a vertex link.

The triangulation
\mathcal{T}
gives
P
the structure of a simplicial complex, and using this we can compute the homology group
H_{2}(P,\mathbb{R})
. Let
C_{2}(P)
be the 2-dimensional chain group, which is generated by oriented triangular faces of the two-skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
.

Remark 12
.

The homology map for closed 3–manifolds in
[
3
]
uses the fact that a closed normal immersion only has finitely many discs and hence one can homotope a transversely oriented immersion in the direction of the transverse orientation into the 2–skeleton. Small triangles map to vertices, quadrilaterals to edges, and the homology map thus
essentially factors through a projection onto the coordinates of the large triangles. Here, we construct a different map using only the quadrilateral coordinates — we map a quadrilateral dual to an edge of a 3–simplex to the two faces of the simplex containing that edge, taken with appropriate signs. It is interesting to note that this new map can also be used for closed 3–manifolds. The upshot is that one can determine the homology class of a closed transversely oriented normal immersion in a closed 3–manifold from the small triangles alone, from the large triangles alone, and from the quadrilaterals alone. In particular, if such a surface represents a non-trivial homology class, it must have at least one disc of each type.

Let
\Delta^{3}
be a tetrahedron in
\mathcal{T}
, with vertices labeled
v_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq 3
. Recall that
q_{ij}
,
0\leq i<j\leq 3
, is the transversely oriented normal quad that separates the edge
e_{ij}
between
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
from the opposite edge, and such that the transverse orientation on
q_{ij}
points toward
e_{ij}
. Let
t_{i}
be the face of
\Delta^{3}
across from
v_{i}
.
First we define
\varphi\co\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern on all of

The heart of the proof of the following proposition is a construction of Segerman

with a surface made up of twisted squares that appears in work of Yoshida

. The map from transversely oriented twisted squares to oriented triangles is then straightforward.

Proposition 13
.

Let

, with

of

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})
span
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
, and so the images
\phi(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
span
\ker\partial_{2}
, and it follows that
\varphi
is surjective.
∎

4.2
Peripheral homology map

We are now interested in the situation where the homology map factors through the boundary map.

The long exact sequence of the pair
(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}})
gives a homomorphism
\varphi:H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
, which for a surface
S\subset\,\overline{\!{M}}
representing a class
[S]\in H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
, maps
[S]
to
[\partial S]\in H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
. Combined with the homology map, we obtain

\varphi\circ\mathcal{H}:\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})

We also let
\partial:\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
be the sum of the maps
\partial^{+},\partial^{-}:\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})\to H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
defined in
Section

2.4
. That is, we define
\partial(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})=\partial^{+}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})+\partial^{-}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
. The definitions of the maps imply
\varphi\circ\mathcal{H}=\partial.

Using the universal coefficient theorem and Poincaré-Lefschetz duality, we have
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\cong H^{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\cong H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R}).

The standard half-lives half-dies argument implies for homology with rational coefficients that the image of the map
H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
induced by the inclusion map has rank
k,
where
k
is the number of boundary components of
\,\overline{\!{M}}.
Hence
\varphi
is an isomorphism onto its image if
H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
is a surjection.

So assume that
H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
is a surjection.
In particular, we can make an identification
\,\overline{\!{M}}=S_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\mathcal{N}(L)
, where
S_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a rational homology sphere, and
L=L_{1}\cup\dots\cup L_{k}
is a link in
S_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Here,
\mathcal{N}(L)
is an open regular neighbourhood of
L
, and we let
\mathcal{N}(L_{i})
denote the connected component that is a regular neighbourhood of
L_{i}
and
B_{i}
denote the frontier of
\mathcal{N}(L_{i}).

Consider
M_{i}=S_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\mathcal{N}(L_{i}).
Then
\partial M_{i}=B_{i}.

The half-lives half-dies argument implies that there is a basis
\langle\mu_{i},\lambda_{i}\rangle=H_{1}(B_{i},\mathbb{Z})
with the property that
\mu_{i}
maps to an element of infinite order whilst
\lambda_{i}
maps to an element of finite order under the inclusion map to
H_{1}(M_{i},\mathbb{Z}).
In particular,
\lambda_{i}
is uniquely determined up to sign, whilst
\mu_{i}
is only well-defined up to sign and a power of
\lambda_{i}.

It follows from the Mayer-Vietoris sequence that
\mu_{i}
may be chosen as the geometric meridian for
L_{i}
and that
\lambda_{i}
is isotopic to the core curve
L_{i}
of
\mathcal{N}(L_{i}).

The set
\{\mu_{1},\lambda_{1},\dots,\mu_{k},\lambda_{k}\}
is a basis for
H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
.
Let
p:H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})\to\Lambda\subset H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
be the projection to the subspace spanned by
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}
. We emphasise that this depends on the identification of
M
as a link complement in a rational homology sphere.

Suppose the order of
\lambda_{i}
is
n_{i}
in
H_{1}(M_{i},\mathbb{Z}).
A construction due to Stallings
[
21
]
produces a properly embedded, connected, oriented surface
S_{i}
in
M_{i}=S_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\mathcal{N}(L_{i})
with
[\partial S_{i}]=n_{i}\lambda_{i}.
We may isotope this surface to be transverse to all other components
L_{j}
of
L
. Hence we obtain a properly embedded surface
\mathring{S}_{i}=S_{i}\cap\,\overline{\!{M}}
in
\,\overline{\!{M}}
with the property that
\partial\mathring{S}_{i}
is the union of
n_{i}
copies of
\lambda_{i}
on
B_{i}
and a number of meridians on the other boundary components of
\,\overline{\!{M}}.

Hence
p\circ\varphi([\mathring{S}_{i}])=n_{i}\lambda_{i}
. In particular, the subspace in
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
spanned by the classes represented by
\mathring{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathring{S}_{k}
is mapped onto
\Lambda
by
p\circ\varphi.
This implies that
\langle[\mathring{S}_{1}],\ldots,[\mathring{S}_{k}]\rangle
has dimension
k
, and since the dimensions of
\Lambda
and
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
are both
k
,
p\circ\varphi
is an isomorphism. In particular:

H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})=\langle[\mathring{S}_{1}],\ldots,[\mathring{S}_{k}]\rangle\cong\langle\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\rangle=\Lambda

We therefore have an isomorphism
\psi\co\Lambda\to H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces given by

\psi(\lambda_{i})=\frac{1}{n_{i}}[\mathring{S}_{i}]
, and it follows from this discussion that
\mathcal{H}=\psi\circ p\circ\partial.

4.3
The algorithm

For correctness of our algorithm, we use the following result.

Theorem 14

(Walsh)
.

[
28
, Theorem 1.6]

Let
M
be an atoroidal, acylindrical, irreducible, compact 3-manifold with torus cusps, and let
\mathcal{T}
be an ideal triangulation of
M
such that no edge of
\mathcal{T}
is isotopic into a cusp. Let
S\subset M
be a properly embedded, 2-sided, incompressible surface that is not a virtual fiber. Then there is an embedded spun normal surface in
\mathcal{T}
that is properly isotopic to
S
.

For
M
a 3-manifold, let
b_{1}(M)
denote the first Betti number.

Theorem 15
.

Let
\,\overline{\!{M}}
be a 3-manifold with non-empty torus boundary such that
M=\mathrm{int}(\,\overline{\!{M}})
is hyperbolic, and let
\mathcal{T}
be an ideal triangulation of

that is

-efficient. Let

If

, then the convex hull

of

of the Thurston norm on

, where

is the homology map defined in

and given here for completeness.

It follows that

is a multiple of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}(S_{i},\nu_{S_{i}})
, where
S_{i}
is any component of
S
. By minimality of
t
it follows that
S
is connected. Since
\chi(S)=\chi^{\ast}(x)=t\chi^{\ast}(v)<0
, we have
-\chi_{-}(S)=\chi(S)=\chi^{\ast}(x)
. By
[
8
, Corollary 6.18]
, the Thurston norm for embedded surfaces is equal to the norm for singular surfaces, so

\|\mathcal{H}(x)\|\leq\chi_{-}(S)=|\chi(S)|=|\chi^{\ast}(x)|

from which it follows that

\|\mathcal{H}(v)\|=\frac{\|\mathcal{H}(x)\|}{|\chi^{\ast}(v)|}\leq 1

Since
\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}
and
\mathcal{B}
are both convex, to show that
\mathcal{B}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}
it is enough to show that the vertices of
\mathcal{B}
are in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}
. To this end, let
y\in\mathcal{B}
, and let
S
be a norm minimizing transversely oriented surface so that
y=[S]/|\chi(S)|
. Since
b_{1}(M)\geq 2
, vertices of
\mathcal{B}
are not top-dimensional faces, so
S
does not lie over a top-dimensional face. Thus by
[
23
, Theorem 3]
,
S
is not homological to a fiber. Now suppose that
p:N\to M
is a finite cover, and denote the respective Thurston norm balls by
\mathcal{B}_{N}
and
\mathcal{B}_{M}
. Identifying
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}})
with
H^{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}})
,
[
8
, Corollary 6.13]
implies that
p^{\ast}(\mathcal{B}_{M})=\mathcal{B}_{N}\cap p^{\ast}(H^{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}}))
, so vertices of
\mathcal{B}_{M}
do not lift to top-dimensional faces of
\mathcal{B}_{N}
(again using that
b_{1}(M)\geq 2
). It follows then that
S
cannot be homological to a virtual fiber. Thus by
Theorem

14

S
can be isotoped into spun-normal form.

Let
x=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}(S,\nu_{S})
. The ray through
x
intersects
P\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})
in a point
v
, which is admissible since
x
is. The point
v
can be expressed as a convex linear combination of vertices of
P\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle Q\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathcal{T})
, so that we have
v=\sum t_{i}v_{i}
where each
v_{i}
is a vertex and
0\leq t_{i}\leq 1
. For each
v_{i}
, let
\bar{v}_{i}
be the smallest multiple of
v_{i}
that is an integral point. Since
\mathcal{T}
is 0-efficient, none of the
\bar{v}_{i}
are normal 2-spheres. Since
M
is hyperbolic it is
\partial
-incompressible, so any disk in
M
is isotopic into a cusp. It follows that
\chi^{\ast}(\bar{v}_{i})\neq 1
, and consequently that
\chi^{\ast}(v_{i})\leq 0
for all
i
. Since
v
is admissible, each
v_{i}
is also admissible. If
\chi^{\ast}(v_{i})=0
, then
\bar{v}_{i}
is the normal coordinate of a connected, immersed surface of zero Euler characteristic, so
\mathcal{H}(\bar{v}_{i})=0
since
M
is atoroidal.

Let
\hat{v}=\sum t_{i}\hat{v}_{i}
, where
\hat{v}_{i}=v_{i}
if
\chi^{\ast}(v_{i})<0
and
\hat{v}_{i}=0
otherwise. Then
\mathcal{H}(\hat{v})=\mathcal{H}(v)
,
\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})=\chi^{\ast}(v)
and each non-zero
\hat{v}_{i}
is a rational multiple of a point in
B
. If follows that
\hat{v}_{i}/|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})|\in B
, and therefore that
\mathcal{H}(\hat{v}_{i}/|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i}|)=\mathcal{H}(\hat{v}_{i})/|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})|\in\mathcal{H}(B)
.
Since
\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})=\sum t_{i}\cdot\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})
and
\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})<0
for all
i
, we obtain
1=\sum t_{i}\cdot\frac{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})|}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})|}
. Thus we have

\sum t_{i}\cdot\frac{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})|}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})|}\frac{\mathcal{H}(\hat{v}_{i})}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v}_{i})|}=\sum t_{i}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{H}(\hat{v}_{i})}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})|}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}

which implies that

y=\frac{[S]}{|\chi(S)|}=\frac{\mathcal{H}(x)}{|\chi^{\ast}(x)|}=\frac{\mathcal{H}(v)}{|\chi^{\ast}(v)|}=\frac{\mathcal{H}(\hat{v})}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})|}=\sum t_{i}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{H}(\hat{v}_{i})}{|\chi^{\ast}(\hat{v})|}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}

We thus have
\mathcal{B}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}(B)}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

1
, which was stated in
Section

1
.

Proof of
Theorem

1
.

Let
b_{1}(M)
denote the first betti number of
M
. If
b_{1}(M)\geq 2
, then
Theorem

15
provides an algorithm via normal surfaces. Now suppose that
b_{1}(M)=1
. Note that
Theorem

15
fails for 1-dimensional homology because in that case the top-dimensional faces are vertices, and therefore vertices of the norm ball can be virtual fibers, which are not always realizeable as normal surfaces. Also note that for the 1-dimensional case the norm ball is determined by two vertices
\pm t\in\mathbb{R}\cong H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
.

Since
\,\overline{\!{M}}
has non-empty boundary and
b_{1}(M)=1
, it must have a single boundary component
T
. Let
\gamma\in H_{1}(T;\mathbb{Z})
be the homological longitude (i.e., the unique curve trivial in
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{Q})
). The curve
\gamma
is the boundary of a connected surface
S
representing a non-trivial class in
H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\partial\,\overline{\!{M}};\mathbb{R})
. Our goal is to determine
|\chi(S)|=\|[S]\|_{T}
, which we will do by Dehn filling along the boundary of
S
then applying the algorithm for closed manifolds of Cooper–Tillmann
[
3
]
.

Let
\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma}
be the Dehn filling of
\,\overline{\!{M}}
along
\gamma
. Let
\tau
be the order of the torsion subgroup of
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}})
, and let
\tau_{\gamma}
be the order of the torsion subgroup of
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma})
. In order to use the Thurston norm computations for
\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma}
to get the norm of
[S]
, we will need to know how many boundary components
S
has. We claim that
|\partial S|=\tau/\tau_{\gamma}
. This is because if
|\partial S|=k
, then the homological longitude
\gamma
has order
k
in
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}},\mathbb{Z})
, since
k
copies of it bound a 2-cycle, and
\gamma
is killed under Dehn filling. To ensure that Dehn filling does not kill any other part of the torsion subgroup, we can use the Mayer–Vietoris sequence of
M_{\gamma}=M\cup\mathbf{T}
, where
\mathbf{T}
is the filling solid torus. The relevant part of the sequence is

0\to H_{2}(\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma})\xrightarrow{\,\,\partial\,\,}H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}})\xrightarrow{\,\,\varphi\,\,}H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}})\oplus H_{1}(\mathbf{T})\xrightarrow{\,\,\psi\,\,}H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma})

With
\overline{S}
denoting the closed surface in
\,\overline{\!{M}}_{\gamma}
resulting from capping off
\partial S
, we have

\ker\varphi=\im(\partial)=\partial([\overline{S}])=\partial S=k\cdot\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}})\cong\mathbb{Z}\langle\gamma\rangle\times\mathbb{Z}\langle\lambda\rangle

for appropriately chosen
\lambda\in H_{1}(\partial\,\overline{\!{M}})
. Thus

\ker\psi=\im\varphi=\mathbb{Z}\langle(\lambda,-\lambda)\rangle\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}\langle(\gamma,0)\rangle
.
Letting
\psi_{0}
be the restriction of
\psi
to
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}})\oplus\{0\}
, we get
\ker\psi_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, since it must be a subgroup of both
\ker\psi
and
H_{1}(\,\overline{\!{M}})
.

We can now complete the proof. After Dehn filling along
\gamma
,
[
3
]
allows us to find
|\chi(\overline{S})|
, and hence we can compute
\mathrm{genus}(\overline{S})=\mathrm{genus}(S):=g
. Then
|\chi(S)|=2g-2+|\partial S|=2g-2+\tau/\tau_{\gamma}
.
∎

5
Implementation and examples

5.1
Implementation

The algorithm described in
Section

4.3
, along with the algorithm for closed manifolds given in
[
3
]
, has been implemented in the computer program
Tnorm

[
29
]
, available at
https://pypi.org/project/tnorm/
. In its current form,
Tnorm
requires working installations of both
Regina
and
SnapPy
inside
Sage
. We hope to remove the dependence on
Sage
in a future release.

In addition to computing the Thurston norm ball of a hyperbolic 3-manifold,
Tnorm
has several other features that are useful when working with spun-normal surfaces. Most notably
Tnorm
can check for embeddedness, connectedness, and orientability, and can compute the Euler characteristic and boundary slopes of a spun-normal surface. These features are also implemented for transversely oriented spun-normal surfaces. Although Regina can compute boundary slopes of (non-transversely oriented) spun-normal surfaces, none of the other features listed are currently implemented in Regina for spun-normal surfaces. Thus
Tnorm
is useful as a companion to
Regina
if one is interested in studying these surfaces.

To compute the boundary slopes of a spun-normal surface,
Tnorm
uses the basis for
H_{1}(\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
computed by
SnapPy
. If
M=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus L
,
L=\cup_{i}L_{i}
, is a link complement and
SnapPy
has a link diagram for
L
(for example, this is the case if
M
is a link from one of
SnapPy
’s link censuses), then this is the
knot-theoretic basis
. That is, the basis used for the component
L_{i}
will consist of the meridian and the longitude that is trivial in
H_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus L_{i})
. Otherwise, provided a hyperbolic structure is found for
M
,
SnapPy
defaults to a
geometric basis
consisting of the two shortest simple closed curves on each cusp cross-section.

. There is a unique embedded normal surface

by

]
. For a connected component

of

by putting an orientation on some initial quadrilateral

, then iteratively assign compatible orientations to all quadrilaterals adjacent to an oriented quadrilateral, until either all quadrilaterals are oriented, or some quadrilateral is adjacent to two oriented quadrilaterals with incompatible orientations. If successful, we get two transverse orientations
\pm\nu_{i}^{\prime}
for each connected component, and hence
2^{k}
possible orientations on
S^{\prime}
. If among these

orientations on

, then
(S^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
is an embedded representative for

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

. If none of the orientations found match the orientation for

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

, or if
S^{\prime}
is not orientable, then since
S^{\prime}
is the unique embedded normal surface with quadrilateral coordinate
x
,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

cannot have an embedded representative. For if it did, then such a surface would be an orientable embedded normal surface with quadrilateral coordinate
x
but not normally isotopic to
S^{\prime}
, contradicting uniqueness of
S^{\prime}
.

5.2
Examples

Tnorm
can be used as an application with a graphical user interface via the terminal command

$ sage -python -m tnorm.app

or can be imported as a module in a
Sage
session. Here we will demonstrate some of the features of
Tnorm
with some examples, using the command line interface.

We start with the figure-8 knot complement. The initial function
tnorm.load()
will take as input any input that
SnapPy
will accept, as well as
SnapPy
manifolds and triangulations, and
Regina
triangulations. After loading the figure-8 knot complement (a.k.a.,
‘4_1’
), we see that
Tnorm
confirms what we found in
Section

2.5
. Namely, the generator of its second homology (rel boundary) is represented by an immersed thrice-punctured sphere. There is an outward boundary component with slope
2\mu+\lambda
, and two inward boundary components each with slope
-\mu
. Since the outward boundary slope and the negative of the inward boundary are not equal, the union of the ends of the surface cannot be normally homotoped to be embedded. Note that although there are other transversely oriented normal surfaces that represent the same homology class,
Tnorm
will always check for an embedded normal surface to represent the primitive integer point over a vertex of the norm ball.

Both of the above examples are fibered knots, as we would expect given
Theorem

14
, which guarantees that surfaces that are not fibers or virtual fibers can be realized as embedded normal surfaces. In fact, we find that among the first 268 hyperbolic knots in the census of Hoste and Thistlethwaite, there is no fibered knot with fiber that is realized by an embedded normal surface in the census triangulation. On the other hand, it is possible that for some other triangulation the fiber can be normalized. This example gives us some hope that the following question could have a positive answer:

Question 16
.

For a fibered knot complement
M=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus K
or fibered once-cusped hyperbolic 3–manifold
M
, is there always some ideal triangulation of
M
such that the fiber is realized as an embedded spun-normal surface?

As discussed in the proof of
Theorem

1
, when
M
is a knot complement it may happen that the generator of
H_{2}(M;\partial M)
is not realized by
any
norm-minimizing transversely oriented normal surface (immersed or embedded). In fact this is the case for the census knot
K8a16
with the census triangulation. Nonetheless,
Tnorm
is still able to determine the norm ball, which it does by computing the genus of the knot using the method described in the proof of
Theorem

1
:

The above phenomenon appears to be rare—in particular, in the census of ideal triangulations of the first 268 hyperbolic knot complements, only eight have no spun-normal surface realizing a generator of
H_{2}(M;\partial M)
. If a hyperbolic manifold
M
is not the complement of a knot in a homology sphere, then the primitive integer homology class over a Thurston norm ball vertex will always be realized by some embedded normal surface. Norm balls for a few hyperbolic link complements are shown in
Figure

11
. These are produced by
Tnorm
as 3D manipulables, viewable in any modern web browser. The first two, in
Figures

11(a)
and
11(b)
, are three component links (and so their norm balls are 3-dimensional). For these two examples the vertices of the norm balls are labelled by the topological type of the normal surface realizing the primitive integer class lying above the vertex. The norm ball in
Figure

11(c)
is the 4-component census link
L9a55
. The norm ball for this link is 4-dimensional, and the figure shows a Schlegel projection of the 4-dimensional polytope to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. All vertices of the norm ball are realized by normal surfaces that are thrice-punctured spheres, but we have suppressed the labels in this case.

For a non-hyperbolic manifold
M
the Thurston norm may only be a semi-norm and the normalisation result of incompressible surfaces due to Walsh
[
28
]
does not apply. However, if every surface in
M
that is non-trivial in homology has negative Euler characteristic, then the Thurston norm is a genuine norm. If, in addition, all vertices of the norm ball are realizable as immersed normal surfaces, then
Tnorm
will correctly compute the norm ball. In the below example we compute the Thurston norm ball for the non-hyperbolic manifold obtained by Dehn filling the third cusp of
L12n1738
along its longitude. Since the filling is along a boundary slope, the resulting manifold has internal homology, so
Tnorm
uses the simplicial homology map (see
Section

4
).

We finish this section with a demonstration of how
Tnorm
can be used to compute topological properties of spun-normal surfaces enumerated by
Regina
. In the example below, we find that the spun-normal surface
S
is a non-orientable connected surface with two boundary components and Euler characteristic -5. When we take the Haken sum of
S
with the thrice-punctured sphere
R
, we get a connected, non-orientable surface
F
with three boundary components. The
spinning slopes
of these surfaces are the boundary slopes described in
[
24
]
, for which the orientation on a boundary component is determined by the direction that it spins into the cusp, rather than on an orientation of the surface. For an oriented surface such as
R
, we can also compute the
oriented boundary slopes
, for the which the orientation on the boundary is induced by the orientation on the surface.

6
An application

Our proof of
Theorem

2
relies on the following result of Fried
[
6
]
, for which we need some notation. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the set of rational points on a fibered face of a Thurston norm unit ball. For
x\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
F_{x}
be the fiber over
x
, and let
\lambda_{x}
be the dilatation of the associated monodromy. Let
L_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined by
L_{\mathcal{F}}(x)=|\chi(F_{x})|\log(\lambda_{x})
.

Theorem 17

(Fried
[
6
]
)
.

The function
L_{\mathcal{F}}
extends to a continuous convex function on
\mathcal{F}
going to infinity toward the boundary of
\mathcal{F}
.

We prove
Theorem

2
by finding a particular fibered link for which the Thurston unit norm ball has a fibered face containing many fibers. The following proposition describes precisely the properties such a link (and its unit norm ball) needs to have.

Proposition 18
.

Let
M=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus(L=L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup L_{3})
be the complement of a fibered 3-component link, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a fibered face of the Thurston norm unit ball for
M
with vertices
x_{1},x_{2}
and
x_{3}
having the following properties:

(1)

2x_{1},2x_{2}
and
x_{3}
have norm minimizing representatives
S_{1}=\Sigma_{1,2}
,
S_{2}=\Sigma_{1,2}
, and
S_{3}=\Sigma_{1,1}
, respectively.

(2)

S_{1}
and
S_{2}
each have a single boundary component on
L_{1}
, and a single boundary component on
L_{2}
, and no boundary component on
L_{3}
.

(3)

The slopes for
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are distinct on
L_{1}
and on
L_{2}
.

(4)

S_{3}
has no boundary components on
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
, and has one boundary component on
L_{3}
.

Then for any
0<\varepsilon<1
, there is a compact subset
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathbb{R}^{+}K_{\varepsilon}
contains a fiber
S_{g,n}
for every
g\geq 2
and every
n
satisfying
\varepsilon g+2\leq n\leq\frac{g}{\varepsilon}+2
.

Proof.

Suppose we have such a link
L
and fibered face
\mathcal{F}
. We first prove the following:

Claim 1:
For every integer
g\geq 2
, there are positive integers
p,q
such that
p+q=g
,
\gcd(p,q)=1
, and
|p-q|\leq 4
.

Proof of Claim 1:

Given
g
, choose
p
and
q
as follows. If
g=2
, set
p=q=1
. If
g
is odd, let
p=\frac{g-1}{2}+1
and let
q=\frac{g-1}{2}
, so that
q=p+1
and hence
\gcd(p,q)=1
. If
g\equiv 0\mod 4
, choose
p=\frac{g}{2}-1
and
q=\frac{g}{2}+1
, so that
q=p+2
and
p
and
q
are both odd (since
\frac{g}{2}
is even). Then
\gcd(p,q)\mid 2
, but since
p
and
q
are odd we must have
\gcd(p,q)=1
. If
g\equiv 2\mod 4
, choose
p=\frac{g}{2}-2
and
q=\frac{g}{2}+2
, so that
q=p+4
. Then
\gcd(p,q)\mid 4
, but
\frac{g}{2}
is odd, so
p
and
q
must also be odd and hence
\gcd(p,q)=1
is the only possibility, thus concluding the proof of Claim 1.

Fix
0<\varepsilon<1
, and define
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{F}
as follows:

K_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\mathcal{F}\,\,\Big|\,\,x=\frac{px_{1}+qx_{2}+rx_{3}}{|px_{1}+qx_{2}+rx_{3}|},\,\,where\,\,\,\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\leq\frac{p}{r},\frac{q}{r},\frac{p}{q}\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\right\}

Since
H_{2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
are linearly independent, we may identify
\frac{x_{i}}{\|x_{i}\|}
,
i=1,2,3
, with the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so that
\mathcal{F}
is the standard 2-simplex. The conditions in the definition of
K_{\varepsilon}
say that the projection of the ray determined by
x=px_{1}+qx_{2}+rx_{3}
to each coordinate plane has slope bounded away from
0
and
\infty
, so that the intersection of this ray with
\mathcal{F}
stays a bounded distance (in the
\mathbb{R}^{3}
metric) from the boundary of
\mathcal{F}
. It follows that
K_{\varepsilon}
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{F}
.

We first show that
\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathcal{F}
contains a fiber
F=\Sigma_{g,n}
for every
g\geq 2
and
n\geq 3
. To this end, fix such a pair
(g,n)
and choose
p,q
as in Claim 1. Let
S^{\prime}=pS_{1}+qS_{2}
. Since
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
have distinct slopes on
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
and
\gcd(p,q)=1
,
S^{\prime}
has exactly two boundary components (one on
L_{1}
and one on
L_{2}
). Since the Thurston norm is linear over the fibered face we have

2+2\cdot\mathrm{genus}(S^{\prime})-2=|\chi(S^{\prime})|=\|S^{\prime}\|=p\|S_{1}\|+q\|S_{2}\|=2p+2q\implies\mathrm{genus}(S^{\prime})=p+q=g

Letting
F=S^{\prime}+rS_{3}
with
r=n-2
, we compute similarly

2\cdot\mathrm{genus}(F)-2+|\partial F|=|\chi(F)|=\|F\|=\|S^{\prime}\|+r\|S_{3}\|=2p+2q+r

Since the two boundary components of
S^{\prime}
are on
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, and the
r
boundary components of
rS_{3}
are on
L_{3}
(i.e., disjoint from the boundary of
S^{\prime}
),
F
has a total of
r+2=n
boundary components, which in turn implies that the genus of
F
is also
g=p+q
. Since there is only one boundary component on, e.g,
L_{1}
,
F
cannot be a multiple of a fiber, and must therefore be a fiber. Since
p,q,r
are positive,
F
is in the interior of the face
\mathcal{F}
.

Now let
F
be as above, and assume that
n
satisfies
\varepsilon g+2\leq n\leq\frac{g}{\varepsilon}+2
. Since
\varepsilon<1
and
|p-q|\leq 4
, we immediately have
\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\leq\frac{1}{5}\leq\frac{p}{q}\leq 5\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon}
. Using
r=n-2
and
g=p+q
we have

\varepsilon g+2\leq n\leq\frac{g}{\varepsilon}+2\implies\varepsilon\leq\frac{p+q}{r}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\implies\frac{p}{r},\frac{q}{r}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon}

Since
|p-q|\leq 4
we have
p\leq q+4\leq 5q
and
q\leq p+4\leq 5p
. This gives

\varepsilon\leq\frac{p+q}{r}\leq\frac{6p}{r}\implies\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\leq\frac{p}{r}

and similarly for
\frac{q}{r}
. It follows that
F\in\mathbb{R}^{+}K_{\varepsilon}
and the proposition is proved.
∎

Figure 12
:
We successively deplumb Hopf bands from the Seifert surface for the oriented link
L9a46
, until we are left with a single Hopf band.

Lemma 19
.

Let
L
be the census link
L9a46
, pictured in
Figure

12
(upper left). Then
L
is fibered, and at least one face of its Thurston norm unit ball for
\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus L
satisfies the conditions of
Proposition

18
.

Proof.

First we show that
L
is fibered. We will use a criterion of Gabai, given in
[
9
, Theorem 5.1]
, which states that an oriented alternating link
L
is fibered if and only if the surface
R
obtained by Seifert’s algorithm is a plumbing of Hopf bands. This means that
R
can be obtained by taking successive Murasugi 4-sums of Hopf bands. Or, equivalently, that we can successively remove Hopf bands from
R
by desumming, until we obtain a disjoint union of Hopf bands. For a discussion of Murasugi sums see
[
9
]
. Note that for a link, Seifert’s algorithm depends on the orientations of the components. If we choose the wrong orientation on
L
we may get a Seifert surface which is not a plumbing of Hopf bands, but it may be that
L
is fibered with respect to some other orientation. With the orientation shown in the upper left of
Figure

12
, we obtain the Seifert surface
R
shown at upper middle-left of the same figure. In general, a Seifert surface is some number of disks joined by twisted bands. If two such bands join the same two Seifert disks and twist in the same direction, then we say they are
parallel
. We first show the following:

Claim 1:
If
b_{l}
and
b_{r}
are parallel bands of a Seifert surface
R
that are separated by at most one other band
b^{\prime}
, then we can deplumb a Hopf band
H
from
R
in such a way that either
B_{l}
or
B_{r}
is removed from
R
.

Proof of Claim 1:
Refer to
Figure

13
. In this figure we remove
b_{r}
. In general, whether we can remove
b_{r}
or
b_{l}
depends on the direction of the twist of
b_{r}
and
b_{l}
, and on the position of
b^{\prime}
. Since we will only need the case shown in
Figure

13
, we will not discuss the other cases (though they are similar). For our case of interest, we (1) slide
b^{\prime}
out of the way (down and to the left), then (2) slide
b_{r}
into position to be desummed, then (3) deplumb
b_{r}
, and finally we (4) slide
b^{\prime}
back to its original position.

With the claim proved, we can now show that
L
is fibered by following the steps of
Figure

12
. In the first step (after Seifert’s algorithm), we identify three parallel bands, and remove two of them (i.e., we do steps
(2\to 3)
of
Figure

13
twice). In the next two steps we identify three bands
b_{r}
,
b_{l}
, and
b^{\prime}
, and remove
b_{r}
using steps
(1\to 4)
of
Figure

13
. Finally, we remove another parallel band, then untwist the last band. We are left with a Hopf band, as desired.

Finally, the claim that the Thurston norm ball for
\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus L
has a face satisfying the conditions of
Proposition

18
is proved by explicitly computing the norm ball. Using the implementation described in
Section

5
, we find that the norm ball is an octahedron, with vertices
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
as shown in
Figure

11(a)
, in which
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are the respective vertices on the positive
y
- and
z
-axes, and
x_{3}
is the vertex on the positive
x
-axis. The points
2x_{1}
and
2x_{2}
lying over
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are represented by surfaces
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, both of which are twice-puntured tori. The vertex
x_{3}
is represented by a once-punctured torus
S_{3}
. The boundary slopes of
S_{1}
are
[(0,1),(-1,0),(0,0)]
, the boundary slopes of
S_{2}
are
[(-1,0),(0,1),(0,0)]
, and the boundary slopes of
S_{3}
are
[(0,0),(0,0),(0,1)]
. Since every face has vertices
\pm x_{1},\pm x_{2},\pm x_{3}
for some choice of signs, all faces of the octahedron satisfy conditions (1) though (4) of
Proposition

18
. Since the link is fibered, at least one of these faces is a fibered face, so the hypotheses of
Proposition

18
are satisfied.
∎

Figure 13
:
Deplumbing a Hopf band.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

2
.

Proof of
Theorem

2
.

By
Lemma

19
there is a fibered link satisfying the conditions of
Proposition

18
. For
\varepsilon>0
fixed, let
c_{\varepsilon}
be the maximum of Fried’s function
L_{\mathcal{F}}
on
K_{\varepsilon}
. By
Proposition

18
, for every
g\geq 2
and
n
satisfying
\varepsilon g+2\leq n\leq\frac{g}{\varepsilon}+2
,
\mathbb{R}^{+}K_{\varepsilon}
contains an element
x
represented by a fiber
F\cong\Sigma_{g,n}
. Thus

l_{g,n}\leq\log(\lambda_{x})=\frac{L_{\mathcal{F}}(x)}{|\chi(F)|}\leq\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{|\chi(F)|}

This is the desired upper bound.
∎

5
for notes on implementation and more detailed examples.
